Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"The main reason I came in is that I get pulled off track so easily—one Slack ping, a song lyric, or a random idea for another project, and suddenly I’ve lost the thread of the dashboard I was supposed to be finishing.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, yeah—so the main thing is I just get pulled off track really easily. Like I’ll be trying to finish a dashboard, and then one Slack ping comes in, or there’s a song lyric stuck in my head, or I suddenly think of a different project I should start, and then I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing.

And then I spend ages trying to remember where I was, which is frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to avoid it. It’s just… my attention goes somewhere else before I even notice.

Yeah, I do. Like if I’m stuck on a long Zoom call or I’m supposed to just sit and work quietly in one spot, I get this kind of… pressure in my body? Like I need to stand up or pace or mess with something in my hands.

Yeah… I think I do, more than I mean to. Especially when people are brainstorming or explaining something and I suddenly get an idea, I’ll just kind of blurt it out before they’re done.

Yeah, a bit. Like, waiting for my turn isn’t impossible, but I get really antsy. Hackathon check-ins are a good example—everyone’s giving updates one by one, and I’ll be standing there checking the time or shifting my weight, kind of rehearsing what I’m going to say.

Yeah, I do. Like, I can sit down and really intend to read a research article, or watch a UX tutorial for class, and at first I’m like okay, I’m focusing… but then a few minutes later I realize my eyes have just been moving across the page or the video has been playing and I have no idea what I just absorbed.

And then I’m like, wait, what were they teaching again? So yeah, keeping my mind on it for a long stretch is hard unless it’s really immediately interesting or interactive.

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit still through a long Zoom call or quietly work in one place, I start feeling this uncomfortable pressure in my body like I need to get up, pace, or do something with my hands.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even when I try to relax by reading or doing a calm sketching exercise, I end up talking to myself, playing music, checking something, or turning it into a bigger project instead of just being quiet with it.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On days when I don’t have classes or a formal schedule, I still jump from editing a prototype to cleaning my desk to opening a new dataset, like resting feels weird unless I’m actively doing something.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn during hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues makes me really antsy, and I catch myself checking the time, shifting around, or wanting to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: When people are brainstorming, I’ll blurt out my idea or start adding to their Figma board before they’ve finished explaining, and I can tell afterward that I came off as pushy even though I was excited.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers wrote comments about me rushing through worksheets, losing track of directions, and talking over classmates during group work. My parents used to sit beside me for homework because I would wander off or start something else before finishing.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I've lost small freelance contracts and missed out on a portfolio competition because people couldn't rely on me to follow through cleanly. I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like breaking it into steps, or keeping track of deadlines, or keeping my stuff physically organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your mind on a task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like, I can sit down and really intend to read a research article, or watch a UX tutorial for class, and at first I’m like okay, I’m focusing… but then a few minutes later I realize my eyes have just been moving across the page or the video has been playing and I have no idea what I just absorbed.\n\nSometimes I’ll rewind the tutorial, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, the person’s interface setup or how they organized their layers, which is sort of related but not actually the point. And then I’m like, wait, what were they teaching again? So yeah, keeping my mind on it for a long stretch is hard unless it’s really immediately interesting or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with focus and restlessness, or did it start when you were older?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was little, not something that just started recently. Like, in elementary school my teachers would write comments about me rushing through worksheets or losing track of the directions.\n\nI remember group work being kind of… I’d talk over other kids sometimes, not really meaning to. And homework was a whole thing—my parents would sit next to me because if they didn’t, I’d wander off or start doing something else before I finished. So yeah, it feels like it’s always been kind of there.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused problems for you at work, in relationships, or in other areas of your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. It has also made some friends and collaborators hesitant to work with me, even though they like my ideas.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been causing real problems for about a year and five months, since I moved from structured school projects into building my freelance UX and data-visualization portfolio.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your mind on a task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like, I can sit down and really intend to read a research article, or watch a UX tutorial for class, and at first I’m like okay, I’m focusing… but then a few minutes later I realize my eyes have just been moving across the page or the video has been playing and I have no idea what I just absorbed.\n\nSometimes I’ll rewind the tutorial, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, the person’s interface setup or how they organized their layers, which is sort of related but not actually the point. And then I’m like, wait, what were they teaching again? So yeah, keeping my mind on it for a long stretch is hard unless it’s really immediately interesting or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with focus and restlessness, or did it start when you were older?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was little, not something that just started recently. And homework was a whole thing—my parents would sit next to me because if they didn’t, I’d wander off or start doing something else before I finished. I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I’d miss some detail and then people kind of stopped trusting me with the handoff.\n\nAnd there was this portfolio competition I wanted to submit to—well, I had this whole idea for it, and I even made a mood board, which I got weirdly into because I love choosing typography and colors, but then the actual submission parts got away from me and I missed it. So that felt really bad.\n\nWith friends or people I collaborate with, I think they like my ideas, but some of them are hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be reliable all the way through. That’s probably the part that stings the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness in multiple areas of your life, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just one setting. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t totally remember if I was, like, climbing on furniture more than other kids or anything like that. I wasn’t constantly scaling trees, I don’t think.

But I do remember—or I guess now it’s still true—that sitting still feels really hard sometimes. Like if I have to sit through a long Zoom call or work quietly in one spot, I get this uncomfortable pressure in my body, like I need to stand up, pace around, or at least do something with my hands. It’s not even always that I want to leave, it’s just this restless feeling that builds up.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—so the main thing is I just get pulled off track really easily. Like I’ll be trying to finish a dashboard, and then one Slack ping comes in, or there’s a song lyric stuck in my head, or I suddenly think of a different project I should start, and then I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I spend ages trying to remember where I was, which is frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to avoid it. It’s just… my attention goes somewhere else before I even notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like if I’m stuck on a long Zoom call or I’m supposed to just sit and work quietly in one spot, I get this kind of… pressure in my body? Like I need to stand up or pace or mess with something in my hands.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, but it’s uncomfortable. I’ll be trying to look normal on camera, and then I’m thinking about whether I look weird shifting around, and then I notice someone’s bookshelf behind them—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, sitting still for too long is hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, more than I mean to. Especially when people are brainstorming or explaining something and I suddenly get an idea, I’ll just kind of blurt it out before they’re done.\n\nLike with group projects, if someone’s walking through a Figma board, I might start adding comments or moving things around while they’re still explaining the thought behind it, and then afterward I realize, oh, that probably came off really pushy. I’m not trying to take over, I just get excited and it feels like if I don’t say it right then, I’ll lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing things in a group?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. Like, waiting for my turn isn’t impossible, but I get really antsy. Hackathon check-ins are a good example—everyone’s giving updates one by one, and I’ll be standing there checking the time or shifting my weight, kind of rehearsing what I’m going to say.\n\nAnd then sometimes I want to jump in before it’s actually my turn, especially if I think I can answer quickly or if there’s a queue for office hours and I’ve been waiting a while. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude, I just start feeling impatient in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and projects?\n\nPatient: Um… organizing tasks and projects? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes I’ll make a list or open a planner app and it helps for a bit, but then I’ll get caught up choosing colors or making the categories too perfect—actually I spent like an hour once making a Notion page instead of doing the thing, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as “trouble organizing” or just me being kind of messy with my process. Can you say what you mean by organizing? Like breaking it into steps, or keeping track of deadlines, or keeping my stuff physically organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your mind on a task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like, I can sit down and really intend to read a research article, or watch a UX tutorial for class, and at first I’m like okay, I’m focusing… but then a few minutes later I realize my eyes have just been moving across the page or the video has been playing and I have no idea what I just absorbed.\n\nSometimes I’ll rewind the tutorial, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, the person’s interface setup or how they organized their layers, which is sort of related but not actually the point. And then I’m like, wait, what were they teaching again? So yeah, keeping my mind on it for a long stretch is hard unless it’s really immediately interesting or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with focus and restlessness, or did it start when you were older?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was little, not something that just started recently. Like, in elementary school my teachers would write comments about me rushing through worksheets or losing track of the directions.\n\nI remember group work being kind of… I’d talk over other kids sometimes, not really meaning to. And homework was a whole thing—my parents would sit next to me because if they didn’t, I’d wander off or start doing something else before I finished. So yeah, it feels like it’s always been kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused problems for you at work, in relationships, or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like, my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it has caused problems. I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I’d miss some detail and then people kind of stopped trusting me with the handoff.\n\nAnd there was this portfolio competition I wanted to submit to—well, I had this whole idea for it, and I even made a mood board, which I got weirdly into because I love choosing typography and colors, but then the actual submission parts got away from me and I missed it. So that felt really bad.\n\nWith friends or people I collaborate with, I think they like my ideas, but some of them are hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be reliable all the way through. That’s probably the part that stings the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness in multiple areas of your life, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just one setting. Like with school or portfolio stuff, it shows up a lot, and then with freelance-type projects too. I’ve actually lost a couple small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or people felt like they couldn’t count on me for the handoff.\n\nAnd socially… I mean, not like everyone hates working with me or anything, but some friends or collaborators are kind of hesitant to do projects with me now. Yeah, it does bother me. I mean, I try not to make it dramatic, because I know people have bigger problems, but it’s embarrassing when people like my ideas and then still don’t fully trust me to finish things cleanly.

And then I’m like, wait, what were they teaching again? So yeah, keeping my mind on it for a long stretch is hard unless it’s really immediately interesting or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with focus and restlessness, or did it start when you were older?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was little, not something that just started recently. Like, in elementary school my teachers would write comments about me rushing through worksheets or losing track of the directions.\n\nI remember group work being kind of… I’d talk over other kids sometimes, not really meaning to. And homework was a whole thing—my parents would sit next to me because if they didn’t, I’d wander off or start doing something else before I finished. So yeah, it feels like it’s always been kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused problems for you at work, in relationships, or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like, my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it has caused problems. I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I’d miss some detail and then people kind of stopped trusting me with the handoff.\n\nAnd there was this portfolio competition I wanted to submit to—well, I had this whole idea for it, and I even made a mood board, which I got weirdly into because I love choosing typography and colors, but then the actual submission parts got away from me and I missed it. So that felt really bad.\n\nWith friends or people I collaborate with, I think they like my ideas, but some of them are hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be reliable all the way through. That’s probably the part that stings the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness in multiple areas of your life, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just one setting. Like with school or portfolio stuff, it shows up a lot, and then with freelance-type projects too. I’ve actually lost a couple small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or people felt like they couldn’t count on me for the handoff.\n\nAnd socially… I mean, not like everyone hates working with me or anything, but some friends or collaborators are kind of hesitant to do projects with me now. Which is frustrating because they’ll say they like my ideas, and I’ll get really excited about the concept—like with this portfolio competition, I made this whole mood board and got super into the colors and typography—but then the actual submission part got away from me and I missed it. So yeah, it affects more than just one area.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often run, climb, or engage in other physically active behavior more than your peers?\n\nPatient: I don’t totally remember if I was, like, climbing on furniture more than other kids or anything like that. I wasn’t constantly scaling trees, I don’t think.\n\nBut I do remember—or I guess now it’s still true—that sitting still feels really hard sometimes. Like if I have to sit through a long Zoom call or work quietly in one spot, I get this uncomfortable pressure in my body, like I need to stand up, pace around, or at least do something with my hands. It’s not even always that I want to leave, it’s just this restless feeling that builds up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember all of it perfectly, but my mom kept some of my old elementary school report cards and the teacher comments were kind of… consistent. Like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to slow down and follow directions,” stuff like that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. Not always like in a classroom “answer before the teacher finishes” way, but more in conversations or brainstorming.

Like if someone’s explaining an idea, especially in design stuff, I’ll suddenly see where it could go and I’ll jump in with my thought before they’re actually done. Or I’ll start adding something to their Figma board while they’re still talking, because in my head I’m like, “Oh, I know what you mean, let me just show it,” but then afterward I realize I kind of bulldozed the moment.

It’s not meant to be pushy. I’m usually just excited, but yeah, I can tell it comes across that way sometimes.

Like breaking it into steps, or keeping track of deadlines, or keeping my stuff physically organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your mind on a task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like, I can sit down and really intend to read a research article, or watch a UX tutorial for class, and at first I’m like okay, I’m focusing… but then a few minutes later I realize my eyes have just been moving across the page or the video has been playing and I have no idea what I just absorbed.\n\nSometimes I’ll rewind the tutorial, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, the person’s interface setup or how they organized their layers, which is sort of related but not actually the point. And then I’m like, wait, what were they teaching again? So yeah, keeping my mind on it for a long stretch is hard unless it’s really immediately interesting or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with focus and restlessness, or did it start when you were older?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was little, not something that just started recently. Like, in elementary school my teachers would write comments about me rushing through worksheets or losing track of the directions.\n\nI remember group work being kind of… I’d talk over other kids sometimes, not really meaning to. And homework was a whole thing—my parents would sit next to me because if they didn’t, I’d wander off or start doing something else before I finished. So yeah, it feels like it’s always been kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused problems for you at work, in relationships, or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like, my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it has caused problems. I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I’d miss some detail and then people kind of stopped trusting me with the handoff.\n\nAnd there was this portfolio competition I wanted to submit to—well, I had this whole idea for it, and I even made a mood board, which I got weirdly into because I love choosing typography and colors, but then the actual submission parts got away from me and I missed it. So that felt really bad.\n\nWith friends or people I collaborate with, I think they like my ideas, but some of them are hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be reliable all the way through. That’s probably the part that stings the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness in multiple areas of your life, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just one setting. Like with school or portfolio stuff, it shows up a lot, and then with freelance-type projects too. I’ve actually lost a couple small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or people felt like they couldn’t count on me for the handoff.\n\nAnd socially… I mean, not like everyone hates working with me or anything, but some friends or collaborators are kind of hesitant to do projects with me now. Which is frustrating because they’ll say they like my ideas, and I’ll get really excited about the concept—like with this portfolio competition, I made this whole mood board and got super into the colors and typography—but then the actual submission part got away from me and I missed it. So yeah, it affects more than just one area.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often run, climb, or engage in other physically active behavior more than your peers?\n\nPatient: I don’t totally remember if I was, like, climbing on furniture more than other kids or anything like that. I wasn’t constantly scaling trees, I don’t think.\n\nBut I do remember—or I guess now it’s still true—that sitting still feels really hard sometimes. Like if I have to sit through a long Zoom call or work quietly in one spot, I get this uncomfortable pressure in my body, like I need to stand up, pace around, or at least do something with my hands. It’s not even always that I want to leave, it’s just this restless feeling that builds up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. I mean, I try not to make it dramatic, because I know people have bigger problems, but it’s embarrassing when people like my ideas and then still don’t fully trust me to finish things cleanly.\n\nLike, I’ve lost a couple of small freelance contracts because the follow-through wasn’t reliable enough, and I missed this portfolio competition I really cared about. I had the fun part started—visual ideas, mood board, all of that—and then the actual submitting part just… didn’t happen in time.\n\nAnd with friends or collaborators, it stings because it’s not that they think I’m untalented. It’s more like they hesitate to work with me because they don’t know if I’ll come through. So yeah, it’s definitely caused distress.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child, similar to the issues you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember all of it perfectly, but my mom kept some of my old elementary school report cards and the teacher comments were kind of… consistent. Like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to slow down and follow directions,” stuff like that.\n\nAnd during group work I guess I’d talk over other kids sometimes. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like I’d get excited and jump in before they were done. I actually hated those little group posters because one person would always want to use glitter glue and then it would get everywhere—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut yeah, my parents used to sit next to me when I did homework because otherwise I’d wander off, or start sharpening pencils, or suddenly decide I needed to reorganize something, and then the homework just wouldn’t get finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the question has been completely asked in social conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always like in a classroom “answer before the teacher finishes” way, but more in conversations or brainstorming.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining an idea, especially in design stuff, I’ll suddenly see where it could go and I’ll jump in with my thought before they’re actually done. Or I’ll start adding something to their Figma board while they’re still talking, because in my head I’m like, “Oh, I know what you mean, let me just show it,” but then afterward I realize I kind of bulldozed the moment.\n\nIt’s not meant to be pushy. I’m usually just excited, but yeah, I can tell it comes across that way sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Or I’ll start adding something to their Figma board while they’re still talking, because in my head I’m like, “Oh, I know what you mean, let me just show it,” but then afterward I realize I kind of bulldozed the moment.\n\nIt’s not meant to be pushy. I’m usually just excited, but yeah, I can tell it comes across that way sometimes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you engage in a lot of physical activity, like running or climbing, when you were a child?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Feeling_Restless\nmanifestation: \"If I have to sit still through a long Zoom call or quietly work in one place, I start feeling this uncomfortable pressure in my body like I need to get up, pace, or do something with my hands.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t remember being especially like, the kid climbing bookshelves or sprinting around constantly—maybe I did sometimes, but what I notice more now is the sitting-still thing.

Like if I’m stuck in a long Zoom call or I’m supposed to quietly work in one place for a while, I get this weird pressure in my body, like I need to stand up or pace or do something with my hands. It’s not even always that I want to go exercise, it’s more like I can’t comfortably stay still. Does that count as the same kind of thing?

Especially when people are brainstorming or explaining something and I suddenly get an idea, I’ll just kind of blurt it out before they’re done.\n\nLike with group projects, if someone’s walking through a Figma board, I might start adding comments or moving things around while they’re still explaining the thought behind it, and then afterward I realize, oh, that probably came off really pushy. I’m not trying to take over, I just get excited and it feels like if I don’t say it right then, I’ll lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing things in a group?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. Like, waiting for my turn isn’t impossible, but I get really antsy. Hackathon check-ins are a good example—everyone’s giving updates one by one, and I’ll be standing there checking the time or shifting my weight, kind of rehearsing what I’m going to say.\n\nAnd then sometimes I want to jump in before it’s actually my turn, especially if I think I can answer quickly or if there’s a queue for office hours and I’ve been waiting a while. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude, I just start feeling impatient in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and projects?\n\nPatient: Um… organizing tasks and projects? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes I’ll make a list or open a planner app and it helps for a bit, but then I’ll get caught up choosing colors or making the categories too perfect—actually I spent like an hour once making a Notion page instead of doing the thing, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as “trouble organizing” or just me being kind of messy with my process. Can you say what you mean by organizing? Like breaking it into steps, or keeping track of deadlines, or keeping my stuff physically organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your mind on a task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like, I can sit down and really intend to read a research article, or watch a UX tutorial for class, and at first I’m like okay, I’m focusing… but then a few minutes later I realize my eyes have just been moving across the page or the video has been playing and I have no idea what I just absorbed.\n\nSometimes I’ll rewind the tutorial, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, the person’s interface setup or how they organized their layers, which is sort of related but not actually the point. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to compare it to other kids, honestly. I don’t have a clear memory of being, like, dramatically more active than everyone else.

But I guess even now, when I don’t have classes or a real schedule, I don’t naturally just relax. I’ll bounce from editing a prototype to cleaning my desk to opening some random dataset, and then somehow it’s been three hours. Resting feels kind of weird unless I’m still doing something. So maybe that was true when I was younger too, just not in the obvious running-around way.

Like, I can sit down and really intend to read a research article, or watch a UX tutorial for class, and at first I’m like okay, I’m focusing… but then a few minutes later I realize my eyes have just been moving across the page or the video has been playing and I have no idea what I just absorbed.\n\nSometimes I’ll rewind the tutorial, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, the person’s interface setup or how they organized their layers, which is sort of related but not actually the point. And then I’m like, wait, what were they teaching again? So yeah, keeping my mind on it for a long stretch is hard unless it’s really immediately interesting or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with focus and restlessness, or did it start when you were older?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was little, not something that just started recently. Like, in elementary school my teachers would write comments about me rushing through worksheets or losing track of the directions.\n\nI remember group work being kind of… I’d talk over other kids sometimes, not really meaning to. And homework was a whole thing—my parents would sit next to me because if they didn’t, I’d wander off or start doing something else before I finished. So yeah, it feels like it’s always been kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused problems for you at work, in relationships, or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like, my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it has caused problems. I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I’d miss some detail and then people kind of stopped trusting me with the handoff.\n\nAnd there was this portfolio competition I wanted to submit to—well, I had this whole idea for it, and I even made a mood board, which I got weirdly into because I love choosing typography and colors, but then the actual submission parts got away from me and I missed it. So that felt really bad.\n\nWith friends or people I collaborate with, I think they like my ideas, but some of them are hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be reliable all the way through. That’s probably the part that stings the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness in multiple areas of your life, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just one setting. Like with school or portfolio stuff, it shows up a lot, and then with freelance-type projects too. I’ve actually lost a couple small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or people felt like they couldn’t count on me for the handoff.\n\nAnd socially… I mean, not like everyone hates working with me or anything, but some friends or collaborators are kind of hesitant to do projects with me now. Which is frustrating because they’ll say they like my ideas, and I’ll get really excited about the concept—like with this portfolio competition, I made this whole mood board and got super into the colors and typography—but then the actual submission part got away from me and I missed it. So yeah, it affects more than just one area.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often run, climb, or engage in other physically active behavior more than your peers?\n\nPatient: I don’t totally remember if I was, like, climbing on furniture more than other kids or anything like that. I wasn’t constantly scaling trees, I don’t think.\n\nBut I do remember—or I guess now it’s still true—that sitting still feels really hard sometimes. Like if I have to sit through a long Zoom call or work quietly in one spot, I get this uncomfortable pressure in my body, like I need to stand up, pace around, or at least do something with my hands. It’s not even always that I want to leave, it’s just this restless feeling that builds up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. I mean, I try not to make it dramatic, because I know people have bigger problems, but it’s embarrassing when people like my ideas and then still don’t fully trust me to finish things cleanly.\n\nLike, I’ve lost a couple of small freelance contracts because the follow-through wasn’t reliable enough, and I missed this portfolio competition I really cared about. I had the fun part started—visual ideas, mood board, all of that—and then the actual submitting part just… didn’t happen in time.\n\nAnd with friends or collaborators, it stings because it’s not that they think I’m untalented. It’s more like they hesitate to work with me because they don’t know if I’ll come through. So yeah, it’s definitely caused distress.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child, similar to the issues you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember all of it perfectly, but my mom kept some of my old elementary school report cards and the teacher comments were kind of… consistent. Like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to slow down and follow directions,” stuff like that.\n\nAnd during group work I guess I’d talk over other kids sometimes. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like I’d get excited and jump in before they were done. I actually hated those little group posters because one person would always want to use glitter glue and then it would get everywhere—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut yeah, my parents used to sit next to me when I did homework because otherwise I’d wander off, or start sharpening pencils, or suddenly decide I needed to reorganize something, and then the homework just wouldn’t get finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the question has been completely asked in social conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always like in a classroom “answer before the teacher finishes” way, but more in conversations or brainstorming.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining an idea, especially in design stuff, I’ll suddenly see where it could go and I’ll jump in with my thought before they’re actually done. Or I’ll start adding something to their Figma board while they’re still talking, because in my head I’m like, “Oh, I know what you mean, let me just show it,” but then afterward I realize I kind of bulldozed the moment.\n\nIt’s not meant to be pushy. I’m usually just excited, but yeah, I can tell it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you engage in a lot of physical activity, like running or climbing, when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember being especially like, the kid climbing bookshelves or sprinting around constantly—maybe I did sometimes, but what I notice more now is the sitting-still thing.\n\nLike if I’m stuck in a long Zoom call or I’m supposed to quietly work in one place for a while, I get this weird pressure in my body, like I need to stand up or pace or do something with my hands. So yeah, it affects more than just one area.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often run, climb, or engage in other physically active behavior more than your peers?\n\nPatient: I don’t totally remember if I was, like, climbing on furniture more than other kids or anything like that. I wasn’t constantly scaling trees, I don’t think.\n\nBut I do remember—or I guess now it’s still true—that sitting still feels really hard sometimes. Like if I have to sit through a long Zoom call or work quietly in one spot, I get this uncomfortable pressure in my body, like I need to stand up, pace around, or at least do something with my hands. It’s not even always that I want to leave, it’s just this restless feeling that builds up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. I mean, I try not to make it dramatic, because I know people have bigger problems, but it’s embarrassing when people like my ideas and then still don’t fully trust me to finish things cleanly.\n\nLike, I’ve lost a couple of small freelance contracts because the follow-through wasn’t reliable enough, and I missed this portfolio competition I really cared about. I had the fun part started—visual ideas, mood board, all of that—and then the actual submitting part just… didn’t happen in time.\n\nAnd with friends or collaborators, it stings because it’s not that they think I’m untalented. It’s more like they hesitate to work with me because they don’t know if I’ll come through. So yeah, it’s definitely caused distress.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child, similar to the issues you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember all of it perfectly, but my mom kept some of my old elementary school report cards and the teacher comments were kind of… consistent. Like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to slow down and follow directions,” stuff like that.\n\nAnd during group work I guess I’d talk over other kids sometimes. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like I’d get excited and jump in before they were done. I actually hated those little group posters because one person would always want to use glitter glue and then it would get everywhere—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut yeah, my parents used to sit next to me when I did homework because otherwise I’d wander off, or start sharpening pencils, or suddenly decide I needed to reorganize something, and then the homework just wouldn’t get finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the question has been completely asked in social conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’m usually just excited, but yeah, I can tell it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you engage in a lot of physical activity, like running or climbing, when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember being especially like, the kid climbing bookshelves or sprinting around constantly—maybe I did sometimes, but what I notice more now is the sitting-still thing.\n\nLike if I’m stuck in a long Zoom call or I’m supposed to quietly work in one place for a while, I get this weird pressure in my body, like I need to stand up or pace or do something with my hands. But yeah, it wasn’t only one place.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your life, without any significant periods where they went away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been causing real problems for about a year and five months, since I moved from structured school projects into building my freelance UX and data-visualization portfolio.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say it never went away, exactly. Like, I’ve always had some version of it, but the part where it’s been causing real problems has been more like… about a year and five months.

That’s around when I moved out of having more structured school projects and started trying to build my freelance UX and data-visualization portfolio. Which sounds very organized when I say it like that, but it’s actually been a lot of me trying to make my own deadlines and then getting pulled into the wrong parts of things. So yeah, it’s been consistently an issue since then, at least in a way that’s actually been messing with my life.

It’s just… my attention goes somewhere else before I even notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like if I’m stuck on a long Zoom call or I’m supposed to just sit and work quietly in one spot, I get this kind of… pressure in my body? Like I need to stand up or pace or mess with something in my hands.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, but it’s uncomfortable. I’ll be trying to look normal on camera, and then I’m thinking about whether I look weird shifting around, and then I notice someone’s bookshelf behind them—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, sitting still for too long is hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, more than I mean to. Especially when people are brainstorming or explaining something and I suddenly get an idea, I’ll just kind of blurt it out before they’re done.\n\nLike with group projects, if someone’s walking through a Figma board, I might start adding comments or moving things around while they’re still explaining the thought behind it, and then afterward I realize, oh, that probably came off really pushy. I’m not trying to take over, I just get excited and it feels like if I don’t say it right then, I’ll lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing things in a group?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. Like, waiting for my turn isn’t impossible, but I get really antsy. Hackathon check-ins are a good example—everyone’s giving updates one by one, and I’ll be standing there checking the time or shifting my weight, kind of rehearsing what I’m going to say.\n\nAnd then sometimes I want to jump in before it’s actually my turn, especially if I think I can answer quickly or if there’s a queue for office hours and I’ve been waiting a while. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude, I just start feeling impatient in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and projects?\n\nPatient: Um… organizing tasks and projects? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes I’ll make a list or open a planner app and it helps for a bit, but then I’ll get caught up choosing colors or making the categories too perfect—actually I spent like an hour once making a Notion page instead of doing the thing, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as “trouble organizing” or just me being kind of messy with my process. Can you say what you mean by organizing? Like breaking it into steps, or keeping track of deadlines, or keeping my stuff physically organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your mind on a task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like, I can sit down and really intend to read a research article, or watch a UX tutorial for class, and at first I’m like okay, I’m focusing… but then a few minutes later I realize my eyes have just been moving across the page or the video has been playing and I have no idea what I just absorbed.\n\nSometimes I’ll rewind the tutorial, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, the person’s interface setup or how they organized their layers, which is sort of related but not actually the point. And then I’m like, wait, what were they teaching again? So yeah, keeping my mind on it for a long stretch is hard unless it’s really immediately interesting or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with focus and restlessness, or did it start when you were older?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was little, not something that just started recently. Like, in elementary school my teachers would write comments about me rushing through worksheets or losing track of the directions.\n\nI remember group work being kind of… I’d talk over other kids sometimes, not really meaning to. And homework was a whole thing—my parents would sit next to me because if they didn’t, I’d wander off or start doing something else before I finished. So yeah, it feels like it’s always been kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused problems for you at work, in relationships, or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like, my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it has caused problems. I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I’d miss some detail and then people kind of stopped trusting me with the handoff.\n\nAnd there was this portfolio competition I wanted to submit to—well, I had this whole idea for it, and I even made a mood board, which I got weirdly into because I love choosing typography and colors, but then the actual submission parts got away from me and I missed it. So that felt really bad.\n\nWith friends or people I collaborate with, I think they like my ideas, but some of them are hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be reliable all the way through. That’s probably the part that stings the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness in multiple areas of your life, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just one setting. Like with school or portfolio stuff, it shows up a lot, and then with freelance-type projects too. I’ve actually lost a couple small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or people felt like they couldn’t count on me for the handoff.\n\nAnd socially… I mean, not like everyone hates working with me or anything, but some friends or collaborators are kind of hesitant to do projects with me now. Which is frustrating because they’ll say they like my ideas, and I’ll get really excited about the concept—like with this portfolio competition, I made this whole mood board and got super into the colors and typography—but then the actual submission part got away from me and I missed it. So yeah, it affects more than just one area.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often run, climb, or engage in other physically active behavior more than your peers?\n\nPatient: I don’t totally remember if I was, like, climbing on furniture more than other kids or anything like that. I wasn’t constantly scaling trees, I don’t think.\n\nBut I do remember—or I guess now it’s still true—that sitting still feels really hard sometimes. Like if I have to sit through a long Zoom call or work quietly in one spot, I get this uncomfortable pressure in my body, like I need to stand up, pace around, or at least do something with my hands. It’s not even always that I want to leave, it’s just this restless feeling that builds up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. I mean, I try not to make it dramatic, because I know people have bigger problems, but it’s embarrassing when people like my ideas and then still don’t fully trust me to finish things cleanly.\n\nLike, I’ve lost a couple of small freelance contracts because the follow-through wasn’t reliable enough, and I missed this portfolio competition I really cared about. I had the fun part started—visual ideas, mood board, all of that—and then the actual submitting part just… didn’t happen in time.\n\nAnd with friends or collaborators, it stings because it’s not that they think I’m untalented. So yeah, it’s definitely caused distress.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child, similar to the issues you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to slow down and follow directions,” stuff like that.\n\nAnd during group work I guess I’d talk over other kids sometimes. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like I’d get excited and jump in before they were done. I actually hated those little group posters because one person would always want to use glitter glue and then it would get everywhere—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut yeah, my parents used to sit next to me when I did homework because otherwise I’d wander off, or start sharpening pencils, or suddenly decide I needed to reorganize something, and then the homework just wouldn’t get finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the question has been completely asked in social conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always like in a classroom “answer before the teacher finishes” way, but more in conversations or brainstorming.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining an idea, especially in design stuff, I’ll suddenly see where it could go and I’ll jump in with my thought before they’re actually done. Like I’ll be trying to finish a dashboard, and then one Slack ping comes in, or there’s a song lyric stuck in my head, or I suddenly think of a different project I should start, and then I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I spend ages trying to remember where I was, which is frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to avoid it. It’s just… my attention goes somewhere else before I even notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like if I’m stuck on a long Zoom call or I’m supposed to just sit and work quietly in one spot, I get this kind of… pressure in my body? Like I need to stand up or pace or mess with something in my hands.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, but it’s uncomfortable. I’ll be trying to look normal on camera, and then I’m thinking about whether I look weird shifting around, and then I notice someone’s bookshelf behind them—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, sitting still for too long is hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, more than I mean to. Especially when people are brainstorming or explaining something and I suddenly get an idea, I’ll just kind of blurt it out before they’re done.\n\nLike with group projects, if someone’s walking through a Figma board, I might start adding comments or moving things around while they’re still explaining the thought behind it, and then afterward I realize, oh, that probably came off really pushy. I’m not trying to take over, I just get excited and it feels like if I don’t say it right then, I’ll lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing things in a group?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. Like, waiting for my turn isn’t impossible, but I get really antsy. Hackathon check-ins are a good example—everyone’s giving updates one by one, and I’ll be standing there checking the time or shifting my weight, kind of rehearsing what I’m going to say.\n\nAnd then sometimes I want to jump in before it’s actually my turn, especially if I think I can answer quickly or if there’s a queue for office hours and I’ve been waiting a while. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude, I just start feeling impatient in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and projects?\n\nPatient: Um… organizing tasks and projects? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes I’ll make a list or open a planner app and it helps for a bit, but then I’ll get caught up choosing colors or making the categories too perfect—actually I spent like an hour once making a Notion page instead of doing the thing, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as “trouble organizing” or just me being kind of messy with my process. Like, in elementary school my teachers would write comments about me rushing through worksheets or losing track of the directions.\n\nI remember group work being kind of… I’d talk over other kids sometimes, not really meaning to. Not like, my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it has caused problems. I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I’d miss some detail and then people kind of stopped trusting me with the handoff.\n\nAnd there was this portfolio competition I wanted to submit to—well, I had this whole idea for it, and I even made a mood board, which I got weirdly into because I love choosing typography and colors, but then the actual submission parts got away from me and I missed it. So that felt really bad.\n\nWith friends or people I collaborate with, I think they like my ideas, but some of them are hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be reliable all the way through. Like I need to stand up or pace or mess with something in my hands.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, but it’s uncomfortable. I’ll be trying to look normal on camera, and then I’m thinking about whether I look weird shifting around, and then I notice someone’s bookshelf behind them—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, sitting still for too long is hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, more than I mean to. Especially when people are brainstorming or explaining something and I suddenly get an idea, I’ll just kind of blurt it out before they’re done.\n\nLike with group projects, if someone’s walking through a Figma board, I might start adding comments or moving things around while they’re still explaining the thought behind it, and then afterward I realize, oh, that probably came off really pushy. I’m not trying to take over, I just get excited and it feels like if I don’t say it right then, I’ll lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when doing things in a group?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. Like, waiting for my turn isn’t impossible, but I get really antsy. Hackathon check-ins are a good example—everyone’s giving updates one by one, and I’ll be standing there checking the time or shifting my weight, kind of rehearsing what I’m going to say.\n\nAnd then sometimes I want to jump in before it’s actually my turn, especially if I think I can answer quickly or if there’s a queue for office hours and I’ve been waiting a while. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude, I just start feeling impatient in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and projects?\n\nPatient: Um… organizing tasks and projects? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes I’ll make a list or open a planner app and it helps for a bit, but then I’ll get caught up choosing colors or making the categories too perfect—actually I spent like an hour once making a Notion page instead of doing the thing, which is probably not the point.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as “trouble organizing” or just me being kind of messy with my process. Can you say what you mean by organizing? Like breaking it into steps, or keeping track of deadlines, or keeping my stuff physically organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your mind on a task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like, I can sit down and really intend to read a research article, or watch a UX tutorial for class, and at first I’m like okay, I’m focusing… but then a few minutes later I realize my eyes have just been moving across the page or the video has been playing and I have no idea what I just absorbed.\n\nSometimes I’ll rewind the tutorial, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, the person’s interface setup or how they organized their layers, which is sort of related but not actually the point. And then I’m like, wait, what were they teaching again? So yeah, keeping my mind on it for a long stretch is hard unless it’s really immediately interesting or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with focus and restlessness, or did it start when you were older?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there since I was little, not something that just started recently. Like, in elementary school my teachers would write comments about me rushing through worksheets or losing track of the directions.\n\nI remember group work being kind of… I’d talk over other kids sometimes, not really meaning to. And homework was a whole thing—my parents would sit next to me because if they didn’t, I’d wander off or start doing something else before I finished. So yeah, it feels like it’s always been kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused problems for you at work, in relationships, or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like, my whole life is falling apart or anything, but it has caused problems. I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I’d miss some detail and then people kind of stopped trusting me with the handoff.\n\nAnd there was this portfolio competition I wanted to submit to—well, I had this whole idea for it, and I even made a mood board, which I got weirdly into because I love choosing typography and colors, but then the actual submission parts got away from me and I missed it. So that felt really bad.\n\nWith friends or people I collaborate with, I think they like my ideas, but some of them are hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be reliable all the way through. That’s probably the part that stings the most.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness in multiple areas of your life, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just one setting. Like with school or portfolio stuff, it shows up a lot, and then with freelance-type projects too. I’ve actually lost a couple small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or people felt like they couldn’t count on me for the handoff.\n\nAnd socially… I mean, not like everyone hates working with me or anything, but some friends or collaborators are kind of hesitant to do projects with me now. Which is frustrating because they’ll say they like my ideas, and I’ll get really excited about the concept—like with this portfolio competition, I made this whole mood board and got super into the colors and typography—but then the actual submission part got away from me and I missed it. So yeah, it affects more than just one area.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often run, climb, or engage in other physically active behavior more than your peers?\n\nPatient: I don’t totally remember if I was, like, climbing on furniture more than other kids or anything like that. I wasn’t constantly scaling trees, I don’t think.\n\nBut I do remember—or I guess now it’s still true—that sitting still feels really hard sometimes. Like if I have to sit through a long Zoom call or work quietly in one spot, I get this uncomfortable pressure in my body, like I need to stand up, pace around, or at least do something with my hands. It’s not even always that I want to leave, it’s just this restless feeling that builds up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others have caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. I mean, I try not to make it dramatic, because I know people have bigger problems, but it’s embarrassing when people like my ideas and then still don’t fully trust me to finish things cleanly.\n\nLike, I’ve lost a couple of small freelance contracts because the follow-through wasn’t reliable enough, and I missed this portfolio competition I really cared about. I had the fun part started—visual ideas, mood board, all of that—and then the actual submitting part just… didn’t happen in time.\n\nAnd with friends or collaborators, it stings because it’s not that they think I’m untalented. It’s more like they hesitate to work with me because they don’t know if I’ll come through. So yeah, it’s definitely caused distress.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child, similar to the issues you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember all of it perfectly, but my mom kept some of my old elementary school report cards and the teacher comments were kind of… consistent. Like, “rushes through worksheets,” or “needs to slow down and follow directions,” stuff like that.\n\nAnd during group work I guess I’d talk over other kids sometimes. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like I’d get excited and jump in before they were done. I actually hated those little group posters because one person would always want to use glitter glue and then it would get everywhere—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut yeah, my parents used to sit next to me when I did homework because otherwise I’d wander off, or start sharpening pencils, or suddenly decide I needed to reorganize something, and then the homework just wouldn’t get finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the question has been completely asked in social conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not always like in a classroom “answer before the teacher finishes” way, but more in conversations or brainstorming.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining an idea, especially in design stuff, I’ll suddenly see where it could go and I’ll jump in with my thought before they’re actually done. Or I’ll start adding something to their Figma board while they’re still talking, because in my head I’m like, “Oh, I know what you mean, let me just show it,” but then afterward I realize I kind of bulldozed the moment.\n\nIt’s not meant to be pushy. I’m usually just excited, but yeah, I can tell it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you engage in a lot of physical activity, like running or climbing, when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember being especially like, the kid climbing bookshelves or sprinting around constantly—maybe I did sometimes, but what I notice more now is the sitting-still thing.\n\nLike if I’m stuck in a long Zoom call or I’m supposed to quietly work in one place for a while, I get this weird pressure in my body, like I need to stand up or pace or do something with my hands. It’s not even always that I want to go exercise, it’s more like I can’t comfortably stay still. Does that count as the same kind of thing?\n\nDoctor: Did you engage in more physical activity than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to compare it to other kids, honestly. I don’t have a clear memory of being, like, dramatically more active than everyone else.\n\nBut I guess even now, when I don’t have classes or a real schedule, I don’t naturally just relax. I’ll bounce from editing a prototype to cleaning my desk to opening some random dataset, and then somehow it’s been three hours. Resting feels kind of weird unless I’m still doing something. So maybe that was true when I was younger too, just not in the obvious running-around way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus, restlessness, or interrupting others in multiple settings, such as at school, at home, or with friends, for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I can remember it showing up really early, not just now.\n\nIn elementary school, teachers would write those comments on my report cards like, “rushes through worksheets,” or that I missed directions because I didn’t stay with them the whole time. And during group work I’d talk over other kids sometimes, which is embarrassing to think about now.\n\nAt home it was kind of the same with homework. My parents would literally sit next to me because if they didn’t, I’d wander off or start doing something else before I finished. I’d be halfway through something and then suddenly be sharpening pencils or looking for a snack or—sorry, that’s probably too much detail. But yeah, it wasn’t only one place.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your life, without any significant periods where they went away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it never went away, exactly. Like, I’ve always had some version of it, but the part where it’s been causing real problems has been more like… about a year and five months.\n\nThat’s around when I moved out of having more structured school projects and started trying to build my freelance UX and data-visualization portfolio. Which sounds very organized when I say it like that, but it’s actually been a lot of me trying to make my own deadlines and then getting pulled into the wrong parts of things. So yeah, it’s been consistently an issue since then, at least in a way that’s actually been messing with my life.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others caused significant problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, unfortunately. Not like my whole life is collapsing or anything, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nI’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly, or I’d be late with parts of it, or the handoff would be messier than it should’ve been. And there was this portfolio competition I really wanted to submit to—I had ideas for it and everything—but I didn’t get it together in time, which still bothers me.\n\nIt also affects people wanting to work with me. Like, friends or collaborators will say they like my ideas, and they do, but I can tell some of them hesitate because they’re not sure I’ll be reliable. Which is fair, I guess, but it feels pretty awful.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and interrupting others caused problems in your romantic relationships?\n\nPatient: Um… romantic relationships, I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really had anything serious enough where I can clearly say it caused major problems, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ve had crushes and talking stages and all that, but that gets messy for normal reasons too, not just because of me. Sometimes I overthink texts—actually, sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking. I guess I haven’t noticed a clear pattern there the way I have with school or work-type stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
